<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the Boone–Higman conjecture. A finitely generated group has decidable word problem if and only if it embeds in a finitely presented simple group.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every finitely generated group $G$, the following are equivalent:* 1. *$G$ has decidable word problem.* 2. *$G$ embeds in a finitely presented simple group.
  - Explanation: Characterizes word-problem decidability for finitely generated groups by embedding into a finitely presented simple group.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-envelope)): Every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in a finitely presented group $\Gamma$ admitting a faithful two-transitive action on a countably infinite set. Every point stabilizer of this action is finitely generated.
  - Explanation: Builds a finitely presented permutation envelope whose faithful action has a countably infinite carrier and finitely generated point stabilizers.
- **C3** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-group-input)): Let $G$ be finitely generated with decidable word problem. There is a finitely generated group $\widehat G$ with decidable word problem containing $G\times G$, and an element $s\in\widehat G$, such that $$[(g,g),s]=(g,1)\qquad(g\in G).$$
  - Explanation: Uses an effective HNN extension to retain the input group and express its elements as controlled commutators.
- **C4** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-algebra-package)): For every finitely generated group $L$ with decidable word problem, there are the following data over $k=\mathbb F_2$:* 1. *A nonzero finitely presented unital algebra $B$, a simple subalgebra $A\subseteq B$ with identity $e$, and an embedding $L\hookrightarrow A^\times$.* 2. *An injective nonunital algebra homomorphism $\phi:B\to B$ with $\phi(B)\subseteq A$ and ${p=\phi(1_B)\ne1_B}$.* 3. *Elements $s_0,s_1,t_0,t_1\in pBp$ commuting with $\phi(B)$ and satisfying $$t_i s_j=\delta_{ij}p,\qquad s_0t_0+s_1t_1=p \quad(i,j\in\{0,1\}).$$* 4. *An element $b_*\in B$ with the following property. Put $$R=B\otimes_k B^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad \psi=\phi\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{op}}, \qquad (a\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}})\cdot m=amb.$$ Then $$r\ne0\ \Longrightarrow\ \psi(r)\cdot b_*\ne0 \qquad(r\in R).$$
  - Explanation: Supplies a finitely presented algebra, a simple subalgebra, a proper-support self-embedding, commuting splitting pairs, and a separating coefficient detector.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-zaremsky)): Suppose a finitely presented group $\Lambda$ acts faithfully on a nonempty countable set $X$. If the point stabilizers are finitely generated and there are finitely many orbits on the unordered two-element subsets of $X$, then $\Lambda$ embeds in a nontrivial finitely presented simple group.
  - Explanation: Gives the action criterion that produces a nontrivial finitely presented simple overgroup.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-cyclic-module)): Let $Y$ be a finite algebra generating set for $B$. For $b\in B$ put $$d_b=b\otimes1-1\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}}, \qquad J=\sum_{y\in Y}R d_y.$$ Evaluation at $1_B$ induces an isomorphism of left $R$-modules $R/J\cong B$.
  - Explanation: Identifies the translation module as a cyclic quotient of the coefficient ring by a finitely generated left ideal.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-presentation)): Let $R$ be a finitely generated unital associative ring, let $J=\sum_{d\in D}Rd$ for a finite subset $D\subset R$, and let $n\geq4$. If $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ is finitely presented, then $$(R/J)^n\rtimes\operatorname{St}_n(R)$$ is finitely presented, where the action on columns is induced by $\pi_n$. More precisely, choose a finite ring generating set $F\subset R$ containing $1$. A finite presentation is obtained from a finite presentation of $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ by adjoining $p_1,\ldots,p_n$ and the relations $$\begin{aligned} &=1,\label{eq:affine-p}\\ [x_{ab}(s),p_c]&=1 &&(s\in F,\ c\ne b),\label{eq:affine-central}\\ [x_{ab}(1),p_b]&=p_a,\label{eq:affine-unit}\\ [x_{ab}(d),p_b]&=1 &&(d\in D).\label{eq:affine-ideal} \end{aligned}$$ Here every root has $a\ne b$, and each root occurring in these finitely many relations is represented by a word in the chosen generators of $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$.
  - Explanation: Presents the affine semidirect product using finitely many translation and elementary-action relations.
- **C8** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-affine)): For the algebra $B$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a>, the group $$\Gamma_0=B^6\rtimes\operatorname{St}_6(R),\qquad R=B\otimes_kB^{\mathrm{op}},$$ is finitely presented.
  - Explanation: Combines the ring presentation and cyclic-module presentation to obtain a finitely presented affine Steinberg group.
- **C9** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-transition)): The formulas $$\theta(x_{ab}(r))=x_{ab}(\psi(r)),\qquad \Theta(m,h)=(\phi^{(6)}(m),\theta(h))$$ define endomorphisms of $\operatorname{St}$ and $\Gamma_0$, respectively. Here $\phi^{(6)}$ acts componentwise on $B^6$. The induced map on elementary matrices is the injective homomorphism $$\label{eq:matrix-transition} F:\operatorname{E}\longrightarrow\operatorname{E}, \qquad F(g)=I+\psi^{(6)}(g-I),$$ where $\psi^{(6)}$ means entrywise application on $6\times6$ matrices. Thus $\pi\theta=F\pi$.
  - Explanation: Defines compatible group and module transitions; the modified-identity matrix formula remains multiplicative for nonunital coefficient maps.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-kernel)): Let $R$ be a unital associative ring, let $\psi:R\to R$ be an additive multiplicative map, and put $P=\psi(1)$. Suppose that $U_0,U_1,V_0,V_1\in PRP$ commute with $\psi(R)$ and satisfy $$V_iU_j=\delta_{ij}P,\qquad U_0V_0+U_1V_1=P.$$ Then the homomorphism $$\theta:\operatorname{St}_6(R)\longrightarrow\operatorname{St}_6(R), \qquad x_{ij}(a)\longmapsto x_{ij}(\psi(a)),$$ satisfies $\theta(\ker\pi)=1$.
  - Explanation: Annihilates the matrix kernel using splitting pairs that commute with the coefficient image, without assuming that the original kernel is central.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-external-roots)): Let $J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,6\}$ and $k\notin J$. For a column $c$ and a row $r$ indexed by $J$, put $$C_k(c)=\prod_{i\in J}x_{ik}(c_i),\qquad R_k(r)=\prod_{i\in J}x_{ki}(r_i).$$ If $w$ is a root word using only indices in $J$, and $A$ is its matrix on those indices, then $$\label{eq:external-action} wC_k(c)w^{-1}=C_k(Ac),\qquad wR_k(r)w^{-1}=R_k(rA^{-1}).$$ Consequently, an element represented by a root word omitting index $6$ and having identity matrix is central in $\operatorname{St}_6(R)$.
  - Explanation: Calculates conjugation of external column and row roots; an unused index detects centrality of identity-matrix root words.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-corner-centrality)): Let $E,F\in R$ be idempotents with $EF=FE=0$. Suppose $d,d^*\in R$ satisfy $$\label{eq:corner-equivalence} d=FdE,\qquad d^*=Ed^*F,\qquad d^*d=E,\qquad dd^*=F.$$ Every root word whose coefficients lie in $ERE$ and whose matrix is the identity represents a central element of $\operatorname{St}_6(R)$.
  - Explanation: Compresses identity-matrix root words into a support omitting an index when an equivalent orthogonal corner is available.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-corner-comparison)): Let $\psi:R\to R$ be an additive multiplicative map. Let $E,F,d,d^*$ satisfy <a href="#eq:corner-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:corner-equivalence">[eq:corner-equivalence]</a>, with $E,F$ orthogonal idempotents, and suppose all four elements commute with $\psi(R)$. For $z\in\ker\pi$, denote its images under the coefficient maps $a\mapsto\psi(a)E$ and $a\mapsto\psi(a)F$ by $z_E$ and $z_F$. Then $z_E=z_F$, and these elements are central.
  - Explanation: Identifies kernel images in equivalent orthogonal corners by a simultaneous diagonal conjugation and centrality.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-orthogonal-root-images)): If $E,F\in R$ are orthogonal idempotents, then the subgroup of $\operatorname{St}_6(R)$ generated by roots with coefficients in $ERE$ commutes with the subgroup generated by roots with coefficients in $FRF$.
  - Explanation: Shows that orthogonal coefficient corners give commuting root-generated subgroups, including opposite root positions.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-mapping-torus)): Let $Q$ be a group and $f:Q\to Q$ an endomorphism. Write $L=\varinjlim(Q,f)$, with classes $[q,j]$ for $j\geq0$ and the convention $[q,j]=[f(q),j+1]$. The map $$\alpha([q,j])=[q,j+1]$$ is an automorphism of $L$, and $$\langle Q,t\mid t^{-1}qt=f(q)\ (q\in Q)\rangle \cong L\rtimes_\alpha\mathbb Z.$$ If $Q$ is finitely presented, so is this group.
  - Explanation: Describes a mapping torus through a direct limit even when its defining endomorphism is not injective.
- **C16** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-limit)): There are compatible isomorphisms $$\varinjlim(\Gamma_0,\Theta) \cong M_\infty^6\rtimes E_\infty, \qquad \Gamma\cong(M_\infty^6\rtimes E_\infty)\rtimes\mathbb Z.$$ In the second isomorphism, conjugation by $t$ shifts the stage index forward by one on both $M_\infty$ and $E_\infty$.
  - Explanation: Removes the Steinberg matrix kernel in the direct limit and identifies the mapping torus as an affine limit with a stage shift.
- **C17** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-two-transitive-action)): The set $X$ is countably infinite. The action of $\Gamma$ on $X$ is faithful and two-transitive, and all its point stabilizers are finitely generated.
  - Explanation: Uses strict stage growth, the separating coefficient vector, and simplicity of the subalgebra to establish a faithful two-transitive action.
- **C18** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-partial-affine)): The monoid $\mathcal P$ has decidable word problem. Each of its elements is a map $x\mapsto ax+b$ on a nonempty rational tail $[r,\infty)\cap\mathbb Q$, or on all of $\mathbb Q$, where $a=2^m$ for some $m\in\mathbb Z$, and $b,r$ are dyadic rationals.
  - Explanation: Decides equality of partial affine maps through exact slope, intercept, and rational-tail data.
- **C19** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-monoids)): Let $\widehat G$ be a finitely generated group with decidable word problem. There exist a finitely presented monoid $K$ with zero $\Omega\ne1_K$, and a finitely generated monoid $H$ with decidable word problem and zero $\zeta\ne1_H$, with the following properties.* 1. *There is an injective group homomorphism $\widehat G\longrightarrow H^\times$.* 2. *For the monoid $T$ defined above, there is an injective unital monoid homomorphism $\kappa:T\longrightarrow H$ with $\kappa(0_T)=\zeta$.* 3. *For every finite ordered tuple $h_1,\ldots,h_d$ of distinct nonzero elements of $H$, with $d\ge1$, there are $L,R\in H$ such that $$Lh_1R=1_H,\qquad Lh_iR=\zeta\quad(2\le i\le d).$$* 4. *There is an injective multiplicative map $c:H\longrightarrow K$ whose image is disjoint from $\{1_K,\Omega\}$. We write $c_h=c(h)$; this map is not required to preserve identities or zeros.* 5. *There is an element $z\in H$ such that the map $$(\tau,\tau')\longmapsto\kappa(\tau)z\kappa(\tau')$$ is an injection from $(T\setminus\{0_T\})^2$ into $H\setminus\{\zeta\}$.
  - Explanation: Constructs mutually encoding monoids with selectors and a separator; finite presentation and decidable normalization serve distinct roles.
- **C20** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-algebra-embeddings)): The algebra $A_0$ is simple, the algebra $B$ is finitely presented, and there are injective algebra homomorphisms $$i:A_0\longrightarrow B, \qquad \rho:B\otimes_k k[\mathcal P]\longrightarrow A_0.$$ The map $\rho$ is unital. The map $i$ is nonunital and has support identity $e=i(1_{A_0})\ne1_B$.
  - Explanation: Passes to contracted monoid algebras; correcting the non-zero-preserving monoid map gives a nonunital algebra embedding.
- **C21** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-binary-tail-corner)): There is a nonzero idempotent $q\in k[\mathcal P]$ and elements $s'_0,s'_1,t'_0,t'_1\in qk[\mathcal P]q$ such that $$t'_i s'_j=\delta_{ij}q,\qquad s'_0t'_0+s'_1t'_1=q.$$
  - Explanation: Realizes orthogonal equivalent subcorners through dyadic partial affine maps and linear combinations of monoid basis elements.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compiler)): From a program index $\eta$ one can compute a finite monoid presentation $K_\eta$ with a zero letter $\Omega$, a finite presentation alphabet $X_\eta$, and an additive grade $\gamma$ on words, such that $0\le\gamma(x)\le M=2$ for every letter and $\gamma(\Omega)=0$. There is a partial word algorithm $N_\eta$, independent of any budget, with the following properties.* *The alphabet contains letters $\alpha,\mathsf Q,\omega$ and right anchor letters $a_R$ for $a\in\Sigma$, all mutually distinct and different from $\Omega$. For a word $u$, let $u_R$ replace each letter $a$ by $a_R$, and set $$\operatorname{raw}(u)=\alpha\mathsf Q u_R\omega\qquad(u\in\Sigma^*).$$ Unconditionally, the algorithm fixes the empty word, $\Omega$, and every literal word $\operatorname{raw}(u)$, and sends every word containing $\Omega$ to $\Omega$. On an input word $a$, its only calls to $f_\eta$ are on words of length at most $\gamma(a)$; apart from those calls it performs finite computations.* *Suppose that, for some integer $m\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned} &f_\eta(w)\text{ is defined and }\|f_\eta(w)\|\le \|w\| &&(\|w\|\le m),\label{eq:budget-total}\\ &f_\eta(xf_\eta(w)y)=f_\eta(xwy) &&(\|xwy\|\le m).\label{eq:budget-context} \end{aligned}$$ Then $N_\eta$ terminates on all words of grade at most $m$. Each such word is joined to its literal output by a finite derivation in the presentation whose intermediate grades do not exceed the input grade. A single presentation equation, used in any context, preserves the output whenever both endpoints have grade at most $m$. Consequently $$\label{eq:bounded-context} N_\eta(u)=N_\eta(v)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad N_\eta(xuy)=N_\eta(xvy) \quad\text{if }\gamma(xuy),\gamma(xvy)\le m.$$* *If <a href="#eq:budget-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-total">[eq:budget-total]</a>–<a href="#eq:budget-context" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget-context">[eq:budget-context]</a> hold for all $m$, then $N_\eta$ decides equality in $K_\eta$ and $$\label{eq:raw-product} N_\eta\bigl(\operatorname{raw}(u)\operatorname{raw}(v)\bigr)=\operatorname{raw}\bigl(f_\eta(uv)\bigr).$$ The presentation is computed without evaluating $f_\eta$.
  - Explanation: Compiles a program index without evaluating it and obtains a partial evaluator with conditional bounded-context guarantees.
- **C23** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-bootstrap)): The function $f$ is total and length-nonincreasing. The rule tables are stable on bounded-length words: for $k\le n$, $\mathcal R_k$ and $\mathcal R_n$ have the same rules whose left sides have length at most $k$. Each $\mathcal R_n$ is confluent on words of length at most $n$, and $$\label{eq:f-context} f(xf(w)y)=f(xwy)\qquad(x,w,y\in\Sigma^*).$$
  - Explanation: Bootstraps totality and context compatibility by a strict decrease in dependency length and stable confluent rule tables.
- **C24** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compiler-components)): Every internal weak component has at most one entry and at most one sector whose head is $q_{\rm ok}$ immediately before its final $\omega$. If both occur, the forward computation from the entry reaches that sector and has the clean form and genuine output described in <a href="#eq:compiler-clean-entry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compiler-clean-entry">[eq:compiler-clean-entry]</a> and <a href="#eq:compiler-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compiler-success">[eq:compiler-success]</a>.
  - Explanation: Controls malformed sectors through finite internal weak components and deterministic transitions with unique entry and accepting terminal behavior.

          "exact_quote": "For every finitely generated group $L$ with decidable word problem, there are the following data over $k=\\mathbb F_2$:*\n\n1.  *A nonzero finitely presented unital algebra $B$, a simple subalgebra $A\\subseteq B$ with identity $e$, and an embedding $L\\hookrightarrow A^\\times$.*\n\n2.  *An injective nonunital algebra homomorphism $\\phi:B\\to B$ with $\\phi(B)\\subseteq A$ and ${p=\\phi(1_B)\\ne1_B}$.*\n\n3.  *Elements $s_0,s_1,t_0,t_1\\in pBp$ commuting with $\\phi(B)$ and satisfying $$t_i s_j=\\delta_{ij}p,\\qquad s_0t_0+s_1t_1=p\n     \\quad(i,j\\in\\{0,1\\}).$$*\n\n4.  *An element $b_*\\in B$ with the following property. Put $$R=B\\otimes_k B^{\\mathrm{op}},\\qquad\n     \\psi=\\phi\\otimes\\phi^{\\mathrm{op}},\n     \\qquad (a\\otimes b^{\\mathrm{op}})\\cdot m=amb.$$ Then $$r\\ne0\\ \\Longrightarrow\\ \\psi(r)\\cdot b_*\\ne0\n     \\qquad(r\\in R).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-250.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "C8",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-prop-affine"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-transition",
        "exact_quote": "The formulas $$\\theta(x_{ab}(r))=x_{ab}(\\psi(r)),\\qquad\n \\Theta(m,h)=(\\phi^{(6)}(m),\\theta(h))$$ define endomorphisms of $\\operatorname{St}$ and $\\Gamma_0$, respectively. Here $\\phi^{(6)}$ acts componentwise on $B^6$. The induced map on elementary matrices is the injective homomorphism $$\\label{eq:matrix-transition}\n F:\\operatorname{E}\\longrightarrow\\operatorname{E},\n \\qquad F(g)=I+\\psi^{(6)}(g-I),$$ where $\\psi^{(6)}$ means entrywise application on $6\\times6$ matrices. Thus $\\pi\\theta=F\\pi$.",
        "source_id": "PM-250.1"
      },

Thus $\\pi\\theta=F\\pi$.",

Here $\\phi^{(6)}$ acts componentwise on $B^6$. Thus $\\pi\\theta=F\\pi$.",
      "explanation": "Defines compatible group and module transitions; the modified-identity matrix formula remains multiplicative for nonunital coefficient maps.",

      "hypotheses": [

      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-mapping-torus",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Q$ be a group and $f:Q\\to Q$ an endomorphism. Write $L=\\varinjlim(Q,f)$, with classes $[q,j]$ for $j\\geq0$ and the convention $[q,j]=[f(q),j+1]$. The map $$\\alpha([q,j])=[q,j+1]$$ is an automorphism of $L$, and $$\\langle Q,t\\mid t^{-1}qt=f(q)\\ (q\\in Q)\\rangle\n \\cong L\\rtimes_\\alpha\\mathbb Z.$$ If $Q$ is finitely presented, so is this group.",

        "exact_quote": "The monoid $\\mathcal P$ has decidable word problem. Each of its elements is a map $x\\mapsto ax+b$ on a nonempty rational tail $[r,\\infty)\\cap\\mathbb Q$, or on all of $\\mathbb Q$, where $a=2^m$ for some $m\\in\\mathbb Z$, and $b,r$ are dyadic rationals.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-monoids",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\widehat G$ be a finitely generated group with decidable word problem. There exist a finitely presented monoid $K$ with zero $\\Omega\\ne1_K$, and a finitely generated monoid $H$ with decidable word problem and zero $\\zeta\\ne1_H$, with the following properties.*\n\n1.  *There is an injective group homomorphism $\\widehat G\\longrightarrow H^\\times$.*\n\n2.  *For the monoid $T$ defined above, there is an injective unital monoid homomorphism $\\kappa:T\\longrightarrow H$ with $\\kappa(0_T)=\\zeta$.*\n\n3.  *For every finite ordered tuple $h_1,\\ldots,h_d$ of distinct nonzero elements of $H$, with $d\\ge1$, there are $L,R\\in H$ such that $$Lh_1R=1_H,\\qquad Lh_iR=\\zeta\\quad(2\\le i\\le d).$$*\n\n4.  *There is an injective multiplicative map $c:H\\longrightarrow K$ whose image is disjoint from $\\{1_K,\\Omega\\}$. We write $c_h=c(h)$; this map is not required to preserve identities or zeros.*\n\n5.  *There is an element $z\\in H$ such that the map $$(\\tau,\\tau')\\longmapsto\\kappa(\\tau)z\\kappa(\\tau')$$ is an injection from $(T\\setminus\\{0_T\\})^2$ into $H\\setminus\\{\\zeta\\}$.",

        "exact_quote": "From a program index $\\eta$ one can compute a finite monoid presentation $K_\\eta$ with a zero letter $\\Omega$, a finite presentation alphabet $X_\\eta$, and an additive grade $\\gamma$ on words, such that $0\\le\\gamma(x)\\le M=2$ for every letter and $\\gamma(\\Omega)=0$. There is a partial word algorithm $N_\\eta$, independent of any budget, with the following properties.*\n\n*The alphabet contains letters $\\alpha,\\mathsf Q,\\omega$ and right anchor letters $a_R$ for $a\\in\\Sigma$, all mutually distinct and different from $\\Omega$. For a word $u$, let $u_R$ replace each letter $a$ by $a_R$, and set $$\\operatorname{raw}(u)=\\alpha\\mathsf Q u_R\\omega\\qquad(u\\in\\Sigma^*).$$ Unconditionally, the algorithm fixes the empty word, $\\Omega$, and every literal word $\\operatorname{raw}(u)$, and sends every word containing $\\Omega$ to $\\Omega$. On an input word $a$, its only calls to $f_\\eta$ are on words of length at most $\\gamma(a)$; apart from those calls it performs finite computations.*\n\n\n\n\n*Suppose that, for some integer $m\\ge0$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n &f_\\eta(w)\\text{ is defined and }|f_\\eta(w)|\\le |w|\n       &&(|w|\\le m),\\label{eq:budget-total}\\\\\n &f_\\eta(xf_\\eta(w)y)=f_\\eta(xwy)\n       &&(|xwy|\\le m).\\label{eq:budget-context}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then $N_\\eta$ terminates on all words of grade at most $m$. Each such word is joined to its literal output by a finite derivation in the presentation whose intermediate grades do not exceed the input grade. A single presentation equation, used in any context, preserves the output whenever both endpoints have grade at most $m$. Consequently $$\\label{eq:bounded-context}\n N_\\eta(u)=N_\\eta(v)\\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n N_\\eta(xuy)=N_\\eta(xvy)\n \\quad\\text{if }\\gamma(xuy),\\gamma(xvy)\\le m.$$*\n\n\n*If <a href=\"#eq:budget-total\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:budget-total\">[eq:budget-total]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:budget-context\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:budget-context\">[eq:budget-context]</a> hold for all $m$, then $N_\\eta$ decides equality in $K_\\eta$ and $$\\label{eq:raw-product}\n N_\\eta\\bigl(\\operatorname{raw}(u)\\operatorname{raw}(v)\\bigr)=\\operatorname{raw}\\bigl(f_\\eta(uv)\\bigr).$$ The presentation is computed without evaluating $f_\\eta$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **word problem and decidability** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The word problem for a group with a specified finite generating set asks whether a given word in the generators and their inverses represents the identity. It is *decidable* if an algorithm answers this question for every word.
- **simple group** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A group is *simple* here if it is nontrivial and its only normal subgroups are the identity subgroup and the whole group.
- **faithful and two-transitive action** ([source](#sec:introduction)): An action on a set $X$ is *faithful* if only the identity acts trivially, and *two-transitive* if it is transitive on the ordered pairs of distinct points of $X$.
- **commutator convention** ([source](#sec:reduction)): Our commutator convention is $[a,b]=aba^{-1}b^{-1}$.
- **simple algebra** ([source](#sec:reduction)): A simple algebra means a nonzero algebra with no proper nonzero two-sided ideal.
- **corner** ([source](#sec:reduction)): For an idempotent $q\in B$, the subalgebra $qBq=\{qbq:b\in B\}$ is called a *corner*; its identity is $q$.
- **partial affine monoid** ([source](#pm-h-24)): Let $\mathcal P$ be the monoid of partial maps of $\mathbb Q$ generated by the full affine maps $$u(x)=x/2,\qquad v(x)=(x+1)/2,$$ their inverses, and the partial identities $e_0,e_1$ on $[0,\infty)\cap\mathbb Q$ and $[1,\infty)\cap\mathbb Q$, respectively.
- **contracted monoid algebra** ([source](#pm-h-25)): For any monoid $D$ with zero $0_D\ne1_D$, its contracted monoid algebra $k_0[D]$ has $k$-basis $D\setminus\{0_D\}$. Basis elements multiply as in $D$, with a product equal to $0_D$ interpreted as the zero vector.
- **monoid algebras** ([source](#pm-h-25)): To prove Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a> for a given group $L$, apply Theorem <a href="#thm:monoids" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:monoids">19</a> with $\widehat G=L$ and set $$A_0=k_0[H],\qquad B=k_0[K].$$
- **shortlex** ([source](#pm-h-29)): Words are ordered by *shortlex*: first by length and then lexicographically.
- **raw word** ([source](#stmt-lem-compiler)): For a word $u$, let $u_R$ replace each letter $a$ by $a_R$, and set $$\operatorname{raw}(u)=\alpha\mathsf Q u_R\omega\qquad(u\in\Sigma^*).$$
- **full raw sector** ([source](#pm-h-35)): A sector is *full raw* if it has the form $$\operatorname{raw}(x)=\alpha\mathsf Q x_R\omega, \qquad x\in\Sigma^*.$$
- **entry** ([source](#pm-h-35)): An *entry* has head $\mathsf I$ immediately before clean $\mathsf D_R$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-group-input) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-group-input): Effective HNN reduction and Britton pinch elimination. -> A finitely generated decidable auxiliary group containing controlled commutators.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-compiler) / [proof](#sec:compiler): Finite machine compilation with grade-preserving internal graphs and bounded-context normalization. -> A finite monoid presentation and a conditional partial evaluator.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-bootstrap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-bootstrap): Effective fixed point and simultaneous induction on length; overlap analysis gives confluence. -> A total length-nonincreasing context-compatible normalizer.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-monoids) / [proof](#pm-h-32): Shortlex code normal forms, separated selector envelopes, and raw-word multiplication. -> Mutually encoding monoids with finite-tuple selectors and a separating letter.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-algebra-package) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-binary-tail-corner-2): Contracted monoid algebras, corrected nonunital embedding, binary tail corners, and tensor basis separation. -> Simple subalgebra, finitely presented ambient algebra, proper-support transition, splitting pairs, and detector.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-affine) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-affine): Finite coefficient-ring presentation, cyclic-module quotient, and explicit relative translation presentation. -> A finitely presented affine Steinberg group.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-thm-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orthogonal-root-images-2): Corner compression and interchange followed by orthogonal splitting and idempotent kernel-image comparison. -> Annihilation of the entire matrix kernel under the transition.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-affine-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affine-limit): Compatible direct limits and a mapping torus of the affine endomorphism. -> A finitely presented affine-limit group with a stage shift.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-prop-two-transitive-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-two-transitive-action): Strict stage filtration, separating-vector detection, and elementary operations over the simple subalgebra. -> Faithful two-transitive action with finitely generated point stabilizers.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-thm-envelope) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-two-transitive-action-2): Controlled commutators embedded through balanced elementary diagonals. -> Embedding of the prescribed input group into the envelope.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-two-transitive-action-3): Apply the simple-overgroup action criterion; prove the converse by parallel enumeration of presentation consequences. -> The equivalence between decidable word problem and embedding into a finitely presented simple group.

          "exact_quote": "Let $G$ be finitely generated with decidable word problem. There is a finitely generated group $\\widehat G$ with decidable word problem containing $G\\times G$, and an element $s\\in\\widehat G$, such that $$[(g,g),s]=(g,1)\\qquad(g\\in G).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-250.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "R1",
      "inputs": [
        "Finite generating set and word-problem decider for the input group."
      ],
      "method": "Effective HNN reduction and Britton pinch elimination.",
      "output": "A finitely generated decidable auxiliary group containing controlled commutators.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-group-input"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-compiler",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-compiler",
          "exact_quote": "From a program index $\\eta$ one can compute a finite monoid presentation $K_\\eta$ with a zero letter $\\Omega$, a finite presentation alphabet $X_\\eta$, and an additive grade $\\gamma$ on words, such that $0\\le\\gamma(x)\\le M=2$ for every letter and $\\gamma(\\Omega)=0$. There is a partial word algorithm $N_\\eta$, independent of any budget, with the following properties.*\n\n*The alphabet contains letters $\\alpha,\\mathsf Q,\\omega$ and right anchor letters $a_R$ for $a\\in\\Sigma$, all mutually distinct and different from $\\Omega$. For a word $u$, let $u_R$ replace each letter $a$ by $a_R$, and set $$\\operatorname{raw}(u)=\\alpha\\mathsf Q u_R\\omega\\qquad(u\\in\\Sigma^*).$$ Unconditionally, the algorithm fixes the empty word, $\\Omega$, and every literal word $\\operatorname{raw}(u)$, and sends every word containing $\\Omega$ to $\\Omega$. On an input word $a$, its only calls to $f_\\eta$ are on words of length at most $\\gamma(a)$; apart from those calls it performs finite computations.*\n\n\n\n\n*Suppose that, for some integer $m\\ge0$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n &f_\\eta(w)\\text{ is defined and }|f_\\eta(w)|\\le |w|\n       &&(|w|\\le m),\\label{eq:budget-total}\\\\\n &f_\\eta(xf_\\eta(w)y)=f_\\eta(xwy)\n       &&(|xwy|\\le m).\\label{eq:budget-context}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then $N_\\eta$ terminates on all words of grade at most $m$. Each such word is joined to its literal output by a finite derivation in the presentation whose intermediate grades do not exceed the input grade. A single presentation equation, used in any context, preserves the output whenever both endpoints have grade at most $m$. Consequently $$\\label{eq:bounded-context}\n N_\\eta(u)=N_\\eta(v)\\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n N_\\eta(xuy)=N_\\eta(xvy)\n \\quad\\text{if }\\gamma(xuy),\\gamma(xvy)\\le m.$$*\n\n\n*If <a href=\"#eq:budget-total\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:budget-total\">[eq:budget-total]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:budget-context\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:budget-context\">[eq:budget-context]</a> hold for all $m$, then $N_\\eta$ decides equality in $K_\\eta$ and $$\\label{eq:raw-product}\n N_\\eta\\bigl(\\operatorname{raw}(u)\\operatorname{raw}(v)\\bigr)=\\operatorname{raw}\\bigl(f_\\eta(uv)\\bigr).$$ The presentation is computed without evaluating $f_\\eta$.",
          "source_id": "PM-250.1"
        }
      ],

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-affine**: A partial affine word is represented by its slope, intercept and full or rational-tail domain. Equality compares all these data exactly.
- **I-group**: The group embedding equivalence requires finite generation and a decidable word problem in its constructive direction.
- **I-budget**: The compiler normalization guarantees depend on the bounded totality and contextual compatibility hypotheses; compiling a presentation does not itself prove program totality.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-software**: Use the embedding equivalence to decide arbitrary software correctness or termination. -> No correspondence from arbitrary programs to the specified group word problem is established.
- **A-finite**: Treat finite affine tests as a certificate for the entire embedding construction. -> The auxiliary word checks do not establish the algebra package, kernel annihilation or the infinite action.
- **A-totality**: Infer evaluator termination from the finite presentation compiled from an arbitrary program index. -> The source separates unconditional compilation from termination under program hypotheses.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The partial affine monoid has an explicit dyadic composition procedure. The code checks finite words in this auxiliary monoid; it does not certify the group embedding or the self-referential compiler.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batches of words over u, v, U, V, e0, e1 on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: U and V denote inverses; each word has at most 32 letters; at most 4096 words.
# OUTPUTS: JSON triples of slope, intercept, and inclusive lower domain bound; null means full domain.
# TIME: O(total letters) exact rational operations; bit cost depends on rational size.
# SPACE: O(batch size plus total output bits).
# ARITHMETIC: exact arbitrary precision rational arithmetic.
# FAILURE: invalid letters, malformed JSON or exceeded bounds raise an exception.
import sys, json
from flint import fmpq
Q = fmpq
generators = {'u': (Q(1,2), Q(0), None), 'v': (Q(1,2), Q(1,2), None),
              'U': (Q(2), Q(0), None), 'V': (Q(2), Q(-1), None),
              'e0': (Q(1), Q(0), Q(0)), 'e1': (Q(1), Q(0), Q(1))}
def compose(left, right):
    a, b, r = left
    c, d, s = right
    threshold = None if r is None else (r-d)/c
    cuts = [x for x in (s, threshold) if x is not None]
    bound = max(cuts) if cuts else None
    return a*c, a*d+b, bound
def normalize(word):
    assert isinstance(word, list) and len(word) <= 32
    current = (Q(1), Q(0), None)
    for letter in word:
        current = compose(current, generators[letter])
    return [str(x) if x is not None else None for x in current]
batch = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
assert isinstance(batch, list) and len(batch) <= 4096
print(json.dumps([normalize(w) for w in batch]))
```

    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON batches of words over u, v, U, V, e0, e1 on stdin.\n# PRECONDITIONS: U and V denote inverses; each word has at most 32 letters; at most 4096 words.\n# OUTPUTS: JSON triples of slope, intercept, and inclusive lower domain bound; null means full domain.\n# TIME: O(total letters) exact rational operations; bit cost depends on rational size.\n# SPACE: O(batch size plus total output bits).\n# ARITHMETIC: exact arbitrary precision rational arithmetic.\n# FAILURE: invalid letters, malformed JSON or exceeded bounds raise an exception.\nimport sys, json\nfrom flint import fmpq\nQ = fmpq\ngenerators = {'u': (Q(1,2), Q(0), None), 'v': (Q(1,2), Q(1,2), None),\n              'U': (Q(2), Q(0), None), 'V': (Q(2), Q(-1), None),\n              'e0': (Q(1), Q(0), Q(0)), 'e1': (Q(1), Q(0), Q(1))}\ndef compose(left, right):\n    a, b, r = left\n    c, d, s = right\n    threshold = None if r is None else (r-d)/c\n    cuts = [x for x in (s, threshold) if x is not None]\n    bound = max(cuts) if cuts else None\n    return a*c, a*d+b, bound\ndef normalize(word):\n    assert isinstance(word, list) and len(word) <= 32\n    current = (Q(1), Q(0), None)\n    for letter in word:\n        current = compose(current, generators[letter])\n    return [str(x) if x is not None else None for x in current]\nbatch = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())\nassert isinstance(batch, list) and len(batch) <= 4096\nprint(json.dumps([normalize(w) for w in batch]))\n",
    "test_vectors": [

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Applicable only to software implementing exact equality for the specified partial affine monoid; the source provides its representation and composition law. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The action is on an abstract countably infinite set; no game-state or simulation model is established. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | An exact symbolic equality checker can implement the stated partial affine monoid procedure. A general program-analysis tool is not supplied. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The partial affine monoid has an explicit exact composition and equality procedure. The HNN reduction additionally assumes a supplied group word-problem decider. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The partial affine monoid uses a concrete representation by slope, intercept and domain threshold; the full domain has a separate representation. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The constructions are algebraic and symbolic; no accelerator architecture or execution mapping is established. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Decidable word equality and an abstract faithful action provide no cryptographic hardness or security guarantee. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The stated algorithms decide algebraic equality; no statistical model, training objective or learning guarantee is established. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Shortlex normalization selects algebraic representatives; the paper establishes no optimization objective or operations-research model. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The affine action and stage shifts are algebraic constructions; no communication, failure or replication model is established. |
| other (Symbolic group theory) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | Applicable to symbolic group theory when the input is a finitely generated group supplied with a word-problem decider. The construction provides an embedding into a finitely presented simple group. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The constructive group embedding requires finite generation and a supplied word-problem decider. Blocked use: ['Applying the embedding construction to an arbitrary group or to a software system without the stated group model.']
- **L2** (edge_case): Trivial and finite input groups are included; the simple target must still be nontrivial. Blocked use: ['Discarding trivial inputs or allowing the trivial group as the simple target.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): Finite presentation of an ambient group does not establish finite presentation of its subgroups. Blocked use: ['Transferring finite presentation to the embedded input group.']
- **L4** (formal_scope): The permutation envelope is a mathematical action on a countably infinite set; engineering reachability, authorization and security guarantees are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Interpreting two-transitivity as physical reachability or an access-control guarantee.']
- **L5** (hypothesis): The passage to a simple overgroup requires a faithful action, finite presentation, finitely generated point stabilizers and finitely many pair orbits. Blocked use: ['Dropping stabilizer or orbit hypotheses when invoking the simple-overgroup criterion.']
- **L6** (hypothesis): The coefficient detector is asserted after the transition map; faithfulness of the original coefficient action is not required. Blocked use: ['Assuming the original coefficient action is faithful merely from the detection property.']
- **L7** (hypothesis): The kernel-annihilation result depends on splitting pairs supported in the specified corner and commuting with the coefficient image. Blocked use: ['Inferring kernel annihilation for arbitrary coefficient maps.']
- **L8** (edge_case): The mapping-torus description permits a noninjective defining endomorphism. Blocked use: ['Treating the base group as automatically embedded in its mapping torus.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): The partial-affine equality procedure is restricted to the stated monoid with dyadic coefficients and positive power-of-two slopes. Blocked use: ['Extending this equality procedure to arbitrary partial maps or using it as a general program-equivalence decider.']
- **L10** (approximation): Equality of represented partial-affine maps requires equality of their domains, slopes and intercepts. Blocked use: ['Certifying equality from floating-point agreement or from agreement on sampled points.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): The finite compiler is produced without evaluating the indexed program; evaluator termination remains conditional on the bounded program hypotheses. Blocked use: ['Inferring totality of an arbitrary indexed program from existence of its finite presentation.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): The self-referential construction supplies no advance bound on machine size or working space; practical complexity guarantees are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Treating decidability as an efficient implementation guarantee.']
- **L13** (hypothesis): Finite internal-component search fixes ordered cell bases and boundary flags and excludes quota insertion. Blocked use: ['Using finite grade alone to claim a finite configuration search with quota insertion enabled.']
- **L14** (formal_scope): A finite exact check of partial-affine composition does not certify the infinite embedding, self-reference or simple-overgroup construction. Blocked use: ['Presenting a finite composition test as verification of the whole group embedding theorem.']
- **L15** (formal_scope): The displayed first affine-presentation relation has an absent left-hand side in the supplied text; a complete executable presentation cannot be recovered from that display alone. Blocked use: ['Implementing the displayed finite presentation without independently resolving the absent relation.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 250 (`lean/docs/250.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Finite algebraic envelopes and the Boone–Higman conjecture](../../preprints/Finite-algebraic-envelopes-and-the-Boone-Higman-conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Simple $F_\infty$ overgroups of groups with decidable word problem](../../preprints/Simple-F-infinity-overgroups-of-groups-with-decidable-word-problem-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A universal group of type $F_\infty$](../../preprints/A-universal-group-of-type-F-infinity-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Boone–Higman conjecture characterizes finitely generated groups with decidable word problem by embeddings into finitely presented simple groups. The formalization proves the equivalence in full: a finitely generated group has a computable word problem for a finite generating set exactly when it embeds by an injective homomorphism into a finitely presented simple group.
>
> The formalization proves the higher-finiteness strengthening of the Boone–Higman conjecture: every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds by an injective homomorphism into a nontrivial simple group of type $F_\infty$. Here type $F_\infty$ means that the group has a classifying CW complex with finitely many cells in each dimension. No additional finiteness property of the original group is assumed.
>
> The formalized result constructs one group $H$ of type $F_\infty$ containing every finitely presented group. Here type $F_\infty$ means that $H$ has a classifying space with finitely many cells in each dimension.
>
> The group $H$ is fixed before the groups embedded into it are chosen. No word-problem assumption is imposed, and the classifying space need not be finite-dimensional or have finitely many cells in total. The paper's converse about recursively presented subgroups is not included.

|---|---|
| `Word` | abbrev |
| `evalWord` | def |

| `main` | theorem |

Comparator source `BooneHigman.lean` (1238 bytes, sha256 `8b929009afeb82c0d78ab00f51b714b09e6bf925b8b85c85a52e652f2ff9f8ce`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace FiniteAlgebraicEnvelopes

universe u

abbrev Word (generatorCount : ℕ) := List (Fin generatorCount × Bool)

def evalWord {GroupType : Type u} [Group GroupType] {generatorCount : ℕ}
    (generators : Fin generatorCount → GroupType) (word : Word generatorCount) : GroupType :=
  (word.map fun letter =>
    if letter.2 then generators letter.1 else (generators letter.1)⁻¹).prod

def HasDecidableWordProblem (GroupType : Type u) [Group GroupType] : Prop :=
  ∃ (generatorCount : ℕ) (generators : Fin generatorCount → GroupType),
    Subgroup.closure (Set.range generators) = ⊤ ∧
    ComputablePred (fun word : Word generatorCount => evalWord generators word = 1)

def EmbedsInFinitelyPresentedSimpleGroup (GroupType : Type u) [Group GroupType] : Prop :=
  ∃ (TargetGroup : Type u) (_ : Group TargetGroup),
    Group.IsFinitelyPresented TargetGroup ∧ IsSimpleGroup TargetGroup ∧
    ∃ embedding : GroupType →* TargetGroup, Function.Injective embedding

theorem main (GroupType : Type u) [Group GroupType] [Group.FG GroupType] :
    HasDecidableWordProblem GroupType ↔ EmbedsInFinitelyPresentedSimpleGroup GroupType := by
  sorry

end FiniteAlgebraicEnvelopes

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the Boone–Higman conjecture. A finitely generated group has decidable word problem if and only if it embeds in a finitely presented simple group.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The word problem for a group with a specified finite generating set asks whether a given word in the generators and their inverses represents the identity. It is *decidable* if an algorithm answers this question for every word. A group is *simple* here if it is nontrivial and its only normal subgroups are the identity subgroup and the whole group.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every finitely generated group $G$, the following are equivalent:*

1.  *$G$ has decidable word problem.*

2.  *$G$ embeds in a finitely presented simple group.*

</div>

An embedding throughout means an injective group homomorphism. In the constructive implication, $G$ is supplied with a finite generating set and a word-problem decider; it need not be finitely presented. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the Boone–Higman conjecture positively.

Higman’s embedding theorem characterizes the finitely generated subgroups of finitely presented groups as those admitting a recursively enumerable set of defining relations (Higman 1961) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Higman61](#ref-Higman61)]; see also (Aanderaa and Cohen 1980, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[AanderaaCohen1980](#ref-AanderaaCohen1980)]. Decidability of the word problem is stronger: it supplies an algorithm for both equality and inequality. Boone and Higman characterized this condition by the existence of embeddings $$G\ \leq\ H\ \leq\ K,
 \qquad H\text{ simple},\quad K\text{ finitely presented}$$ (Boone and Higman 1974, Theorem I) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]. They asked whether the simple overgroup $H$ could itself be chosen finitely presented (Boone and Higman 1974, 43) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]. The two properties in their theorem belong to different groups, and finite presentation does not in general pass to subgroups. Thompson subsequently obtained a finitely generated simple overgroup with decidable word problem (Thompson 1980) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Tho80](#ref-Tho80)]; a later proof appears in (Darbinyan and Steenbock 2022, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DarbinyanSteenbock2022](#ref-DarbinyanSteenbock2022)]. This strengthens the conclusion to finite generation of the simple overgroup, but does not supply its finite presentation.

Positive cases of the conjecture include hyperbolic and contracting self-similar groups (Belk, Bleak, et al. 2026, Theorem A and Corollary D) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BBMZhyperbolic](#ref-BBMZhyperbolic)], Baumslag–Solitar groups and finite-rank free-by-cyclic groups (Bux, Llosa Isenrich, and Wu 2025, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BuxLlosaWu2025](#ref-BuxLlosaWu2025)], and automorphism groups of finite-rank free groups and mapping class groups of orientable punctured surfaces of finite type (Belk, Fournier-Facio, et al. 2026, Theorem A and Corollary B) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BFFHZ](#ref-BFFHZ)]. The survey (Belk et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BBMZsurvey](#ref-BBMZsurvey)] describes further results and the development of the conjecture.

Our principal intermediate result concerns permutation actions. An action on a set $X$ is *faithful* if only the identity acts trivially, and *two-transitive* if it is transitive on the ordered pairs of distinct points of $X$.

<a id="stmt-thm-envelope"></a>
<div id="thm:envelope" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in a finitely presented group $\Gamma$ admitting a faithful two-transitive action on a countably infinite set. Every point stabilizer of this action is finitely generated.*

</div>

The action is designed for the twisted Brin–Thompson construction of Belk and Zaremsky, which embeds an acting group in a simple group (Belk and Zaremsky 2022, sec. 3 and Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BelkZaremsky2022](#ref-BelkZaremsky2022)]. Their original finite-presentation theorem used finitely many orbits on finite subsets of each size and finitely presented finite-set stabilizers (Belk and Zaremsky 2022, Theorem D) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BelkZaremsky2022](#ref-BelkZaremsky2022)]. Zaremsky subsequently showed that finite presentation of the acting group, finite generation of point stabilizers, and finitely many orbits on unordered pairs suffice (Zaremsky 2024, Theorem A and Corollary B) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Zaremsky2024Finite](#ref-Zaremsky2024Finite)]. Two-transitivity supplies the last condition here. We state this criterion precisely in Theorem <a href="#thm:zaremsky" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zaremsky">5</a>; the construction below provides its faithful action and the embedding of the prescribed group.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The proof

The action is affine. Translations move the first point of an ordered pair to zero, so two-transitivity reduces to transitivity of the linear part on nonzero vectors. A simple algebra $A$ provides the required coefficient operations: if $0\ne m\in A$, then its identity belongs to $AmA$, and hence is a finite sum of left and right multiples of $m$. Elementary row operations can use this identity to move vectors. Finite presentation, however, will come from a different algebra.

Over $k=\mathbb F_2$, we construct a finitely presented algebra $B$, a simple subalgebra $A\subseteq B$ with its own identity, and an injective nonunital map $\phi:B\to B$ with image in $A$. The additive direct limit $M_\infty=\varinjlim(B,\phi)$ is the carrier for the action on $X=M_\infty^6$. Every finite collection of its coordinates lies in one copy of $B$; advancing once places them in $A$. Thus simplicity becomes available at a later stage without requiring the finitely presented algebra $B$ itself to be simple.

The coefficient ring $R=B\otimes_k B^{\mathrm{op}}$ acts on $B$ by left and right multiplication. We give an explicit finite presentation of the affine Steinberg group $$\Gamma_0=B^6\rtimes\operatorname{St}_6(R).$$ The Steinberg group maps onto the elementary matrix group $\operatorname{E}_6(R)$, but its kernel is an obstruction to the desired faithful affine action. We remove that entire kernel by a single application of the coefficient map induced by $\phi$. The key algebraic input consists of two splitting pairs commuting with the coefficient image. Their corner identities give an exact calculation in the Steinberg presentation; no centrality hypothesis on the kernel is needed.

The resulting endomorphism of $\Gamma_0$ defines a finitely presented mapping torus $\Gamma$. In its direct-limit description the Steinberg kernel has disappeared, leaving elementary matrices acting on $M_\infty^6$, together with translations and a stage shift. Simplicity of $A$ gives the nonzero-vector orbit required for two-transitivity. Faithfulness uses two further properties of the algebra construction: a separating vector detects every nonzero coefficient after one advance, and the proper support $\phi(1_B)\ne1_B$ makes the stage shift detectable. The stabilizer of zero is the quotient by the translation subgroup, so it is finitely generated. To embed the original $G$, we first use an HNN extension to express its elements as controlled commutators; balanced elementary diagonals then place this copy of $G$ in the linear part.

It remains to construct the algebras with these simultaneous properties. We do so through a finitely presented monoid and a decidable monoid that encode one another. A finite presentation is compiled syntactically from a program index, before any totality assertion is available. Its partial evaluator controls the length of every call to the encoded program. Long codes make the calls needed to normalize a word strictly shorter than that word. A fixed-point argument and induction on length then establish totality and compatibility with multiplication. Finite-tuple selectors isolate individual basis terms in a contracted monoid algebra, producing $A$; a separate separator distinguishes ordered pairs of basis terms, producing the faithful coefficient detector.

The finite-presentation construction follows the machine-encoding tradition of Post and Murskii (Post 1947; Murskii 1967) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Post1947](#ref-Post1947), [Murskii1967](#ref-Murskii1967)]. Here the bounded evaluator permits the two monoids to be constructed together. The selectors strengthen the contextual-collapse property in the semigroup counterpart of the Boone–Higman theorem (Boone and Higman 1974, 49–52) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]: isolating each term of a finite linear combination gives the simplicity needed for the affine action.

Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">2</a> states the algebraic data and gives the HNN reduction. Sections <a href="#sec:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine">3</a>–<a href="#sec:action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:action">5</a> construct the action and prove the two theorems from those data. Section <a href="#sec:algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algebra">6</a> constructs the algebras from a precise monoid theorem, proved by bounded self-reference in Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">7</a>. Appendix <a href="#sec:compiler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compiler">8</a> gives the full finite compiler and its evaluator proof.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### The group input and the algebraic data

We first enlarge the input group so that each of its elements is a commutator in the larger group. This will allow us to embed the input group in an elementary matrix group in Section <a href="#sec:action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:action">5</a>. We then state the algebraic construction used in the next three sections. All rings and algebras have identities; a homomorphism explicitly called nonunital is not required to preserve them. Our commutator convention is $[a,b]=aba^{-1}b^{-1}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-group-input"></a>
<div id="lem:group-input" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Let $G$ be finitely generated with decidable word problem. There is a finitely generated group $\widehat G$ with decidable word problem containing $G\times G$, and an element $s\in\widehat G$, such that $$[(g,g),s]=(g,1)\qquad(g\in G).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-group-input"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-group-input</summary>

*Proof.* Take the HNN extension $$\widehat G=
 \left\langle G\times G,s\ \middle|\
 s(g,g)s^{-1}=(1,g)\quad(g\in G)\right\rangle .$$ The associated subgroups are the diagonal and the second factor, with the indicated isomorphism. Britton’s Lemma embeds the base (Britton 1963) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Britton63](#ref-Britton63)]; see also the precise effective HNN formulation in (Boone and Higman 1974, 44) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)].

We spell out the decision procedure. Store each base word as a pair of words on the given generators of $G$. Membership of $(a,b)$ in the diagonal is decided by testing $ab^{-1}=1$, and membership in the second factor by testing $a=1$. Replace each available pinch by $$s(a,a)s^{-1}\longmapsto(1,a),
 \qquad
 s^{-1}(1,b)s\longmapsto(b,b),$$ and multiply adjacent base words. Each pinch removes two stable letters. When no pinch remains, a word still containing a stable letter is nontrivial by Britton’s Lemma. Otherwise decide the resulting base word coordinatewise. Thus the procedure terminates and decides the word problem, without requiring a finite presentation of $G$. The two copies of a finite generating set, together with $s$, generate $\widehat G$. Finally, $$[(g,g),s]=(g,g)\,s(g^{-1},g^{-1})s^{-1}
           =(g,g)(1,g^{-1})=(g,1).$$ This includes finite groups and the trivial group; in the latter case $\widehat G$ is infinite cyclic. ◻

</details>

Here is the algebraic construction. A simple algebra means a nonzero algebra with no proper nonzero two-sided ideal. A subalgebra with identity $e$ need not contain the identity of the ambient algebra; its units are taken relative to $e$. For an idempotent $q\in B$, the subalgebra $qBq=\{qbq:b\in B\}$ is called a *corner*; its identity is $q$.

<a id="stmt-thm-algebra-package"></a>
<div id="thm:algebra-package" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4**. *For every finitely generated group $L$ with decidable word problem, there are the following data over $k=\mathbb F_2$:*

1.  *A nonzero finitely presented unital algebra $B$, a simple subalgebra $A\subseteq B$ with identity $e$, and an embedding $L\hookrightarrow A^\times$.*

2.  *An injective nonunital algebra homomorphism $\phi:B\to B$ with $\phi(B)\subseteq A$ and ${p=\phi(1_B)\ne1_B}$.*

3.  *Elements $s_0,s_1,t_0,t_1\in pBp$ commuting with $\phi(B)$ and satisfying $$t_i s_j=\delta_{ij}p,\qquad s_0t_0+s_1t_1=p
     \quad(i,j\in\{0,1\}).$$*

4.  *An element $b_*\in B$ with the following property. Put $$R=B\otimes_k B^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad
     \psi=\phi\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{op}},
     \qquad (a\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}})\cdot m=amb.$$ Then $$r\ne0\ \Longrightarrow\ \psi(r)\cdot b_*\ne0
     \qquad(r\in R).$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a> is proved in Section <a href="#sec:algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algebra">6</a>, using the monoid construction of Section <a href="#sec:bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bootstrap">7</a> and Appendix <a href="#sec:compiler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compiler">8</a>. To see how these data produce an action, regard the elements of $R$ as coefficients for row operations on $B^6$. Such a coefficient acts on a coordinate by a finite sum of left and right multiplications. Simplicity of $A$ will let a nonzero coordinate produce any prescribed element of $A$; applying $\phi$ places all coordinates in that subalgebra. Finite presentation of $B$ supplies a finitely presented Steinberg group acting by row operations. The splitting pairs make its matrix kernel disappear under the induced coefficient map, while $b_*$ detects every remaining nonidentity matrix after that map is applied. Finally, the support identity $p\ne1_B$ makes successive vector stages strictly larger, so that shifting stages gives a nontrivial permutation. Sections <a href="#sec:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine">3</a>–<a href="#sec:action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:action">5</a> establish these claims and assemble the action.

In particular, $p\phi(b)=\phi(b)p=\phi(b)$, so $1_B\notin\phi(B)$. The detection condition concerns the action after applying $\psi$; it does not require the original action of $R$ on $B$ to be faithful.

<a id="eq:ring-pairs"></a>
Apply the theorem with $L=\widehat G$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:group-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:group-input">3</a>. For the remainder of Sections <a href="#sec:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine">3</a>–<a href="#sec:action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:action">5</a>, fix these data. Tensor products are over $k$. Besides $p$, define $$P=p\otimes p^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad
 U_i=s_i\otimes p^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad
 V_i=t_i\otimes p^{\mathrm{op}}.$$ Tensoring injective linear maps over a field shows that $\psi$ is injective, so $P\ne0$. Also $P\ne1_R$, since the nonzero tensor $(1_B-p)\otimes1_B$ annihilates $P$ on the left. The elements $U_i,V_i$ lie in $PRP$, commute with $\psi(R)$, and satisfy $$\label{eq:ring-pairs}
 V_iU_j=\delta_{ij}P,\qquad U_0V_0+U_1V_1=P.$$ These identities follow directly from the corresponding identities in $B$; the second tensor factor is always the idempotent $p^{\mathrm{op}}$.

The final passage from an action to a simple group uses the following established result, in the precise form needed here.

<a id="stmt-thm-zaremsky"></a>
<div id="thm:zaremsky" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Zaremsky). *Suppose a finitely presented group $\Lambda$ acts faithfully on a nonempty countable set $X$. If the point stabilizers are finitely generated and there are finitely many orbits on the unordered two-element subsets of $X$, then $\Lambda$ embeds in a nontrivial finitely presented simple group.*

</div>

These are the hypotheses of a type-<span class="upright">(A)</span> action in (Zaremsky 2024, Theorem A and Corollary B) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Zaremsky2024Finite](#ref-Zaremsky2024Finite)]. Finite presentation follows from that theorem; the canonical embedding and simplicity are those of the twisted Brin–Thompson construction (Belk and Zaremsky 2022, sec. 3 and Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BelkZaremsky2022](#ref-BelkZaremsky2022)]. Since $X$ is nonempty, the constructed group contains Thompson’s group $V$ on a coordinate and is nontrivial. Our construction will provide a countably infinite $X$ with one orbit on pairs, and will prove each of the other hypotheses separately.

<a id="sec:affine"></a>
### A finitely presented affine group

We now use the algebraic data in Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a> to construct a finitely presented group of translations and elementary operations. The translations form the additive group of $B^6$. We first give their finite presentation relative to a Steinberg group, and then construct the endomorphism used in the next two sections.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The coefficient ring and the translation module

Put $$R=B\otimes_k B^{\mathrm{op}},
 \qquad (a\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}})\cdot m=amb\quad(m\in B).$$ Thus $r=\sum_\nu a_\nu\otimes b_\nu^{\mathrm{op}}$ acts on $B$ by the operation $m\mapsto\sum_\nu a_\nu m b_\nu$. The opposite multiplication in the second factor makes composition of these operations agree with multiplication in $R$, so $B$ is a left $R$-module. The ring $R$ is finitely presented as a unital associative ring: take a finite algebra presentation of $B$, a second copy with products reversed, the relations commuting every generator of the first copy with every generator of the second, and $2\cdot1=0$. The universal property of the tensor product identifies the resulting ring with $R$. The module $B$ is generated by $1_B$; we next show that the relations on this module generator also have a finite description.

<a id="stmt-lem-cyclic-module"></a>
<div id="lem:cyclic-module" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *Let $Y$ be a finite algebra generating set for $B$. For $b\in B$ put $$d_b=b\otimes1-1\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}},
 \qquad J=\sum_{y\in Y}R d_y.$$ Evaluation at $1_B$ induces an isomorphism of left $R$-modules $R/J\cong B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cyclic-module"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cyclic-module</summary>

*Proof.* Evaluation is a surjective $R$-module homomorphism, and every $d_y$ annihilates $1_B$. The identity <a id="eq:module-product"></a>$$\label{eq:module-product}
 d_{bc}=(b\otimes1)d_c+(1\otimes c^{\mathrm{op}})d_b$$ shows, together with additivity and $d_1=0$, that $d_b\in J$ for all $b\in B$. Indeed, $B$ is generated by $Y$ over the prime field $k$. Consequently $$a\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}}\equiv ab\otimes1\pmod J
 \qquad(a,b\in B).$$ Every element of $R$ is therefore congruent modulo $J$ to its evaluation tensored with $1$. The additive map $b\mapsto(b\otimes1)+J$ is an inverse to the induced evaluation map. Thus the kernel is exactly the stated left ideal. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:steinberg-add"></a>
<a id="eq:steinberg-commute"></a>
For a unital ring $R$ and $n\geq3$, the Steinberg group $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ has generators $x_{ab}(r)$, where $1\leq a\ne b\leq n$ and $r\in R$, with relations <a id="eq:steinberg-product"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 x_{ab}(r)x_{ab}(s)&=x_{ab}(r+s),\label{eq:steinberg-add}\\
 [x_{ab}(r),x_{bc}(s)]&=x_{ac}(rs)
       &&(a,b,c\text{ distinct}),\label{eq:steinberg-product}\\
 [x_{ab}(r),x_{cd}(s)]&=1
       &&(a\ne d,\ b\ne c).\label{eq:steinberg-commute}
\end{aligned}$$ Let $e_{ab}$ denote the matrix unit. The map $x_{ab}(r)\mapsto I+r e_{ab}$ gives a surjection $$\pi_n:\operatorname{St}_n(R)\longrightarrow\operatorname{E}_n(R),$$ where $\operatorname{E}_n(R)$ is the subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_n(R)$ generated by these elementary matrices. Krstić and McCool prove that $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ is finitely presented whenever $R$ is a finitely presented unital associative ring and $n\geq4$ (Krstić and McCool 1999, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[KrsticMcCool1999](#ref-KrsticMcCool1999)]. Their theorem permits noncommutative rings, and applies to the ring constructed above.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### A finite relative presentation

The next lemma supplies the additional relations needed for the translations. Finite presentation of the module and of the linear group alone would not establish its conclusion; we prove the semidirect-product presentation explicitly.

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-presentation"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-presentation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:affine-central"></a>
<a id="eq:affine-unit"></a>
<a id="eq:affine-ideal"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *Let $R$ be a finitely generated unital associative ring, let $J=\sum_{d\in D}Rd$ for a finite subset $D\subset R$, and let $n\geq4$. If $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ is finitely presented, then $$(R/J)^n\rtimes\operatorname{St}_n(R)$$ is finitely presented, where the action on columns is induced by $\pi_n$. More precisely, choose a finite ring generating set $F\subset R$ containing $1$. A finite presentation is obtained from a finite presentation of $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ by adjoining $p_1,\ldots,p_n$ and the relations <a id="eq:affine-p"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
&=1,\label{eq:affine-p}\\
 [x_{ab}(s),p_c]&=1
     &&(s\in F,\ c\ne b),\label{eq:affine-central}\\
 [x_{ab}(1),p_b]&=p_a,\label{eq:affine-unit}\\
 [x_{ab}(d),p_b]&=1
     &&(d\in D).\label{eq:affine-ideal}
\end{aligned}$$ Here every root has $a\ne b$, and each root occurring in these finitely many relations is represented by a word in the chosen generators of $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-presentation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-presentation</summary>

*Proof.* Let $Q$ be the group given by this presentation. In the intended semidirect product, $p_a$ translates by $1+J$ in row $a$, and $[x_{ab}(r),p_b]$ translates by $r+J$ in that row. We recover these translations and their elementary action from the finite relations, working inside $Q$ until the final inverse-homomorphism argument.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Coefficients.

Relation <a href="#eq:affine-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-central">[eq:affine-central]</a> holds for every $s\in R$. To see this, let $C$ be the set of coefficients for which it holds for every permitted triple of indices. By <a href="#eq:steinberg-add" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:steinberg-add">[eq:steinberg-add]</a>, $C$ is an additive subgroup containing $F$. If $r,s\in C$, choose $h\notin\{a,b,c\}$. There is such an index because $n\geq4$. Then $$x_{ab}(rs)=[x_{ah}(r),x_{hb}(s)],$$ and both factors commute with $p_c$. Thus $C$ is closed under multiplication, so $C=R$.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Independence of a column.

For $a\ne b$, define $$w_a^b(r)=[x_{ab}(r),p_b].$$ If $h$ differs from $a,b$, then $w_a^b(r)$ commutes with $p_h$: both $x_{ab}(r)$ and $p_b$ do. Put $$X=x_{ah}(r),\qquad Y=x_{hb}(1),\qquad Z=x_{ab}(r).$$ The Steinberg relation $[X,Y]=Z$ gives $XY=ZYX$. Conjugating $p_b$ by these equal products yields $$\begin{aligned}
 (XY)p_b(XY)^{-1}&=w_a^h(r)p_hp_b,\\
 (ZYX)p_b(ZYX)^{-1}&=p_hw_a^b(r)p_b.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $X$ fixes $p_b$, $Y$ sends $p_b$ to $p_hp_b$, and $Z$ fixes $p_h$. Cancelling $p_b$ and using the preceding commutation with $p_h$ proves $w_a^h(r)=w_a^b(r)$. We write this common value as $w_a(r)$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### Commutations.

The element $w_a(r)$ commutes with every $p_c$: choose its defining column $b\notin\{a,c\}$, so that both defining factors commute with $p_c$. It also commutes with $x_{cd}(s)$ whenever $d\ne a$. For this, choose the defining column $b\notin\{a,c,d\}$. Then $x_{ab}(r)$ commutes with $x_{cd}(s)$ by <a href="#eq:steinberg-commute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:steinberg-commute">[eq:steinberg-commute]</a>, and $p_b$ commutes with $x_{cd}(s)$ because $b\ne d$.

These facts imply that all the $w$’s commute. Given $w_a(r)$ and $w_c(s)$, choose $b\notin\{a,c\}$ and write $w_c(s)=[x_{cb}(s),p_b]$. The element $w_a(r)$ commutes with both factors, hence with their commutator. This argument includes $a=c$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Additivity.

Using $[uv,p]=u[v,p]u^{-1}[u,p]$ and any $b\ne a$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 w_a(r+s)
  &=[x_{ab}(r)x_{ab}(s),p_b]\\
  &=x_{ab}(r)w_a(s)x_{ab}(r)^{-1}w_a(r)\\
  &=w_a(r)w_a(s).
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality uses the commutations just proved. Thus $w_a:(R,+)\to Q$ is a homomorphism. Moreover, $w_a(1)=p_a$ by <a href="#eq:affine-unit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-unit">[eq:affine-unit]</a>. In particular, any additive torsion of $R$ is already imposed on these translations; in characteristic two, $p_a^2=w_a(1+1)=1$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Conjugation.

<a id="eq:affine-action"></a>
It remains to determine the action of roots whose column is $a$. For $c\ne a$, choose $b\notin\{a,c\}$ and put $$X=x_{ca}(s),\qquad Y=x_{ab}(r),\qquad Z=x_{cb}(sr).$$ The element $X$ fixes $p_b$, and $XYX^{-1}=ZY$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 Xw_a(r)X^{-1}
    &=[ZY,p_b]
      =Zw_a(r)Z^{-1}w_c(sr)
      =w_a(r)w_c(sr).\label{eq:affine-action}
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality holds because the column $b$ of $Z$ differs from $a$. Together with the commutations already proved, <a href="#eq:affine-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-action">[eq:affine-action]</a> is precisely the elementary action on an additive column over the left module $R$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### The module relations.

Relation <a href="#eq:affine-ideal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-ideal">[eq:affine-ideal]</a> says $w_a(d)=1$ for every $a$ and $d\in D$. To kill a left multiple in a prescribed row $c$, choose $a\ne c$ and conjugate $w_a(d)=1$ by $x_{ca}(s)$. Equation <a href="#eq:affine-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-action">[eq:affine-action]</a> gives $w_c(sd)=1$. Additivity now proves that every $w_c$ vanishes on $J$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Inverse homomorphisms.

Let $P_0=(R/J)^n\rtimes\operatorname{St}_n(R)$. Sending $p_a$ to translation by $1+J$ in row $a$ and using the identity on $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ satisfies all the defining relations of $Q$, and hence gives a homomorphism $f:Q\to P_0$. Conversely, the commuting additive maps $$r+J\longmapsto w_a(r)$$ define a homomorphism $(R/J)^n\to Q$. Equation <a href="#eq:affine-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-action">[eq:affine-action]</a> and the root commutations give the required equivariance, so this map and the canonical map $\operatorname{St}_n(R)\to Q$ induce $g:P_0\to Q$. The composite $gf$ fixes $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ and each $p_a=w_a(1)$, so it is the identity on $Q$. The composite $fg$ fixes $\operatorname{St}_n(R)$ and every translation: in $P_0$, the commutator of $x_{ab}(r)$ with translation by $1+J$ in row $b$ is translation by $r+J$ in row $a$. Thus $f$ and $g$ are inverse isomorphisms. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-affine"></a>
<div id="prop:affine" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8**. *For the algebra $B$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a>, the group $$\Gamma_0=B^6\rtimes\operatorname{St}_6(R),\qquad R=B\otimes_kB^{\mathrm{op}},$$ is finitely presented.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-affine"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-affine</summary>

*Proof.* The ring $R$ is finitely presented, so the Krstić–McCool theorem applies to $\operatorname{St}_6(R)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cyclic-module" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cyclic-module">6</a> identifies $B$ with a quotient of $R$ by a finitely generated left ideal. Lemma <a href="#lem:affine-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affine-presentation">7</a>, with $n=6$, gives the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The transition endomorphism

Write $\operatorname{St}=\operatorname{St}_6(R)$, $\operatorname{E}=\operatorname{E}_6(R)$, and $\pi=\pi_6:\operatorname{St}\to\operatorname{E}$. Recall the injective algebra map $\phi:B\to B$ from Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a>, and set $\psi=\phi\otimes\phi^{\mathrm{op}}:R\to R$. This map is injective because tensoring vector spaces over $k$ preserves injections. Both maps may be nonunital.

<a id="stmt-lem-transition"></a>
<div id="lem:transition" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *The formulas $$\theta(x_{ab}(r))=x_{ab}(\psi(r)),\qquad
 \Theta(m,h)=(\phi^{(6)}(m),\theta(h))$$ define endomorphisms of $\operatorname{St}$ and $\Gamma_0$, respectively. Here $\phi^{(6)}$ acts componentwise on $B^6$. The induced map on elementary matrices is the injective homomorphism <a id="eq:matrix-transition"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-transition}
 F:\operatorname{E}\longrightarrow\operatorname{E},
 \qquad F(g)=I+\psi^{(6)}(g-I),$$ where $\psi^{(6)}$ means entrywise application on $6\times6$ matrices. Thus $\pi\theta=F\pi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transition"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transition</summary>

*Proof.* Additivity and multiplicativity of $\psi$ preserve all the Steinberg relations, proving that $\theta$ is a homomorphism. For an elementary tensor $r=a\otimes b^{\mathrm{op}}$ and $m\in B$, $$\phi(r\cdot m)=\phi(amb)
   =\phi(a)\phi(m)\phi(b)
   =\psi(r)\cdot\phi(m).$$ By additivity this holds for every $r\in R$. Consequently, for each elementary root, $$\phi^{(6)}\bigl((I+r e_{ab})m\bigr)
   =(I+\psi(r)e_{ab})\phi^{(6)}(m).$$ The roots generate $\operatorname{St}$, so this equivariance proves that $\Theta$ is an endomorphism of the semidirect product.

For the matrix assertion, write $g=I+A$ and $h=I+C$. Entrywise application of $\psi$ is additive and multiplicative on matrix rings, even though it does not preserve their identity. Since $gh-I=A+C+AC$, we have $$\begin{aligned}
 F(gh)
  &=I+\psi^{(6)}(A)+\psi^{(6)}(C)
       +\psi^{(6)}(A)\psi^{(6)}(C)\\
  &=F(g)F(h).
\end{aligned}$$ Also $F(I)=I$, and the same calculation shows $F(g^{-1})=F(g)^{-1}$. On elementary matrices, $F(I+r e_{ab})=I+\psi(r)e_{ab}$, proving both that $F$ maps $\operatorname{E}$ into $\operatorname{E}$ and that $\pi\theta=F\pi$. Finally, $F(g)=I$ forces every entry of $g-I$ to vanish because $\psi$ is injective. Thus $F$ is injective. ◻

</details>

We have obtained a finite presentation and compatible transition maps. The map $\Theta$ need not be injective: the remaining obstruction lies in the kernel of $\pi$. The next section proves that $\theta$ annihilates this entire kernel.

<a id="sec:kernel"></a>
### Annihilating the Steinberg kernel

The affine group of Section <a href="#sec:affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine">3</a> uses a Steinberg group, whose matrix action can have a kernel. We now show that the coefficient endomorphism annihilates this entire kernel. The argument uses the splitting pairs from Theorem <a href="#thm:algebra-package" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algebra-package">4</a>, whose elements commute with the coefficient image. It is convenient to state it for an arbitrary unital associative ring.

<a id="eq:kernel-chain"></a>
Recall that $\operatorname{St}_6(R)$ has generators $x_{ij}(a)$, where $1\leq i\ne j\leq6$ and $a\in R$, with relations <a id="eq:kernel-additive"></a><a id="eq:kernel-distant"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 x_{ij}(a)x_{ij}(b)&=x_{ij}(a+b),\label{eq:kernel-additive}\\
 [x_{ij}(a),x_{jk}(b)]&=x_{ik}(ab)
       &&(i,j,k\text{ distinct}),\label{eq:kernel-chain}\\
 [x_{ij}(a),x_{kl}(b)]&=1
       &&(i\ne l,\ j\ne k).\label{eq:kernel-distant}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $[u,v]=uvu^{-1}v^{-1}$. Let $\pi:\operatorname{St}_6(R)\to\operatorname{E}_6(R)$ send $x_{ij}(a)$ to $I+ae_{ij}$.

<a id="stmt-thm-kernel"></a>
<div id="thm:kernel" class="theorem">

**Theorem 10**. *Let $R$ be a unital associative ring, let $\psi:R\to R$ be an additive multiplicative map, and put $P=\psi(1)$. Suppose that $U_0,U_1,V_0,V_1\in PRP$ commute with $\psi(R)$ and satisfy $$V_iU_j=\delta_{ij}P,\qquad U_0V_0+U_1V_1=P.$$ Then the homomorphism $$\theta:\operatorname{St}_6(R)\longrightarrow\operatorname{St}_6(R),
 \qquad x_{ij}(a)\longmapsto x_{ij}(\psi(a)),$$ satisfies $\theta(\ker\pi)=1$.*

</div>

For the binary Leavitt algebra $L=L_{\mathbb F_2}(1,2)$, Khanh proves that the natural map $\operatorname{St}_r(L)\to\mathrm{GL}_r(L)$ is an isomorphism for $r\ge3$ (Khanh 2026, Theorem 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Khanh2026Leavitt](#ref-Khanh2026Leavitt)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:kernel">10</a> concerns an arbitrary ring and annihilation of its matrix kernel under the specified coefficient map.

<a id="eq:kernel-nonunital"></a>
Additivity and multiplicativity make $\theta$ a homomorphism by the defining relations. The map $\psi$ need not preserve the identity. For any additive multiplicative map $\lambda:R\to R$, the corresponding image of a root word with matrix $g$ has matrix $$\label{eq:kernel-nonunital}
 I+\lambda^{(6)}(g-I),$$ where $\lambda^{(6)}$ acts entrywise. This follows on generators and then on products by expanding $gh-I=(g-I)+(h-I)+(g-I)(h-I)$. In particular, coefficient maps preserve the condition of having identity matrix. We shall use this for maps whose images lie in smaller idempotent corners of $R$.
